#6888. Every Outfit TV Is the Digital-Age Fashion Police We Never Knew We Needed

The founders of @everyoutfitonSATC spill the tea on their latest venture into video podcasting

“There are some things that need to be seen to be believed,” says Lauren Garroni, the brunette half of @EveryOutfitonSATC—a Sex and the City fashion archive Instagram account turned Fashion Police-style podcast that she runs with her blonde counterpart, Chelsea Fairless. She’s talking about a stretched canvas image of Dave Navarro walking in the Anna Sui Spring-Summer 1997 runway show, which is currently being sold online by a well-known publishing company. But she might as well have been speaking about the pair’s latest venture: Every Outfit TV.

“Every Outfit is a weekly rundown of the comings and goings in pop culture and fashion,” explains Garroni. She and Fairless launched their podcast in 2021, around the time that the SATC spinoff And Just Like That was announced. Devotees will recall that it was actually phase two, phase one being an Instagram account born in 2016 from Fairless’s frustration with the lack of variety when it came to photos of SATC outfits on the internet. Ten years later, the podcast is viral, the Instagram has 709k followers, and the overall project has morphed into Every Outfit TV, a video component available exclusively on Patreon.

The video format offers Fairless and Garroni the chance to do things that wouldn’t work with solely audio. (Note: not every new podcast episode will have video.) “We grew up being obsessed with the VH1 Vogue Fashion Awards and stuff like House of Style,” says Fairless. The brilliantly lo-fi, green-screen-style opening sequence posted to their Instagram account last week distills the energy they’re going for with its nods to The Nanny, Clueless, and Access Hollywood.

Podcast listeners, TV viewers, and Instagram followers can expect new franchises like “It’s Always Fashion Week Somewhere” and “The Runway Rewind,” which will showcase the pair’s thoughts on runway shows while tapping into the tabloid entertainment vibe that they both admire. There will also be Oprah Winfrey-style book club episodes and “Top 10” lists fiercely curated by Garroni and Fairless themselves.

Ask the duo why now was the ideal time to pivot, and Fairless answers bluntly. “Lauren forced me to,” she says. Garroni sees it differently. “Chelsea finally succumbed to being on camera,” she refutes. Really, the two are responding to a general podcast-world shift to video, a medium that enables them to “fulfill an itch,” says Fairless. She didn’t have cable in her house as a child, prompting her to develop an obsession with fashion magazines and websites like First View (RIP). Garroni’s upbringing makes up for it. “My parents just gave me unrestricted access to television, cable, and the internet from a young age,” she says. Now, both of them are self-diagnosed as chronically online and cite hours on TikTok and Instagram as the reason why they collectively possess the knowledge of someone who has a PhD in fashion history.

While this wealth of niche information could potentially alienate an audience, Garroni and Fairless’s playful banter (think Siskel and Ebert, or Waldorf and Statler) makes up for it. They don’t shy away from cross-generational references, either. “We have laundered our older gay men references that we learned from watching E! News and E! True Hollywood Story,” says Garroni.

In the end, the duo intends to be authentically themselves. “I think Lauren and I are examples of millennials who are very culturally Gen X,” says Fairless. “We were always more interested in Gen X things, Gen X people. I didn’t give a shit about the Backstreet Boys when I was a teenager. We were into Hole and Pavement.”

It would be remiss not to talk about the hit television series that started it all. At this point, they have documented every outfit on Sex and the City more than once in the past ten years, but developments in the show’s universe kept them going. “Something would always happen to prevent us from abandoning this,” says Fairless. Garroni agrees, recalling when Cynthia Nixon ran for governor of New York in 2018. (They released a satirical book We Should All Be Mirandas a year later. Garroni claims to be a Miranda with a Carrie rising, while Fairless is a Miranda with a Samantha rising.) “It always sustained us,” says Garroni. “Every moment we thought, ‘Okay, how long can this joke go on for?’”

If Fairless has anything to do with it, the answer to that question is forever. “Sex and the City is never done. If there is one thing I know to be true in this life, it will never go away,” she says. “It will always change forms and there will always be something newsworthy to talk about.”

Asked what else is coming down the pipeline, Garroni is quick to mention field pieces that will take them out of their impeccably designed Los Angeles set, complete with vintage gray furniture designed in the ’90s by architect David Chipperfield and a pink carpet. (It was modeled after a mashup of Halston’s New York City apartment—an Every Outfit hyperfixation—and the Chicago department store Carson Pirie Scott.) Fairless, however, has other goals in mind. She’d like to add more single-breasted blazers to her wardrobe rotation, she says. Also: “I want hair and makeup.”

#6886. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

#6882. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#6880. Faux Fur, Tuxedo Dressing, and Other Coat Trends Taking Over This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These are the styles to know as the weather gets colder

A great winter outfit deserves an equally great coat, especially when it’s an essential layer for the next several months. Beyond the practical need for warmth, outerwear often sets the tone for an entire look as the first layer you see. A sharply tailored wool coat can make jeans and a sweater feel more polished, while an oversize silhouette or rich texture brings dimension and new proportions to otherwise clean-lined looks.

For fall/winter 2026, designers are rethinking classic outerwear through distinctive silhouettes, textures, and details. At Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi, precise tailoring took a dressier turn, with slim silhouettes, tuxedo-inspired designs, and satin lapels bringing eveningwear polish to wool coats. Meanwhile, Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry embraced leather for a sleeker city-ready take on winter dressing. Faux fur took on a distinctly vintage feel at Stella McCartney, Bottega Veneta, and Khaite, while plush collars added texture to everything from elegant overcoats to more everyday designs.

Whether you’re looking to invest in a forever piece or simply want something fresher than the coat you’ve worn for the past few winters, these six trends offer plenty of inspiration.

Faux fur is taking a distinctly retro turn this winter, with plush textures and elegant silhouettes reminiscent of vintage finds. Stella McCartney’s floor-grazing belted coat and Bottega Veneta’s voluminous fur both recall the glamour of the ’30s and ’40s, while Khaite’s shaggy cream jacket has more of a ’70s feel, particularly when dressed down with jeans. It’s the go-to coat to wear to upcoming holiday parties and formal dinners, or if you just want to upgrade your traditional winter coat for something a bit more luxurious this season.

Think precise cuts, elongated silhouettes, and tuxedo-inspired touches that feel polished enough for an evening out. There’s a more dressed-up sensibility to these classic designs—as seen at Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi—from the precise fit to the smallest finishing details. This attention to detail brings a beautiful sense of sophistication to workwear and when paired with simple everyday separates.

#6877. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6874. How Olivia Munn Takes the Edge Off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The actress shares the beauty and self-care essentials she swears by, including a $75 volumizing cream

Welcome to Take the Edge Off, a series that breaks down the beauty and self-care routines of influencers, CEOs, experts, and celebrities. Find out how they unwind and decompress, and take a closer look at the holy-grail essentials they’re raving about.

Life can throw unexpected surprises—good and bad—your way, and many lean on wellness practices to stay grounded in chaos. Olivia Munn knows this firsthand.

The actress was diagnosed with early-stage Luminal B breast cancer in early 2023, thanks to what she refers to as the “life-saving” risk assessment test with renowned obstetric and gynecologic surgeon Thaïs Aliabadi at the helm. With a toddler and fresh off a double mastectomy, Munn turned to meditation to find her footing through it all.

“I always associated meditation with having to quiet your mind, and I couldn’t imagine how anyone actually did that, but over time, I’ve come to understand that it isn’t about stopping the noise in your head; it’s about learning not to be carried away by it,” Munn tells Bazaar. “I don’t feel the need to chase every thought or immediately make sense of everything. I can just let things settle without feeling responsible for figuring them all out.”

That same strength and balance are exactly what fashion label Anne Klein wanted to embody when the brand enlisted Munn as ambassador for its fall/winter 2026 collection and to continue spreading her message.

Ahead, we caught up with Munn about what else keeps her present during times of stress, from the manifestation candle she loves to light to words she lives by.

“The future you is depending on the you today to do the right thing.” This means the me today depended on the me three years ago to go to the doctor, get the MRI, and face cancer head-on. I was depending on the me last week, month, or year to start working out more, learn that new language, or organize my closet. When I think about how fast time goes by, and how tomorrow, next month, and next year come in the blink of an eye, it motivates me to take the first step toward whatever I want to do.

A couple of months ago, I began using prescription-strength azelaic acid and tretinoin. I start with azelaic acid, then tretinoin, and finish with the Volume Cream by Adipeau, my holy-grail moisturizer.

My favorite cream right now is Adipeau’s Volume Cream. I’ve reached the point in my life where my hands and neck are showing signs of being crepey. A friend told me about this cream and promised me that if I used it every day for a month, I’d see a difference, and she was right. The science behind it is incredible, so incredible that I often worry about whether the company is doing well because I need them to stay in business forever.

My makeup goal has always been no-makeup makeup. But it is incredibly difficult to master, especially on yourself. My hat’s off to all the influencers who do it so beautifully; I see you and wish I were you.

Shige brand cuticle oil. The amazing manicurist Shige [Taylor] created her own nail line, beginning with an incredible cuticle oil made in Japan. [My] nail bed has never looked or felt so hydrated.

Yes, I light candles from Aloha Elixir. They’re made in Hawaii and are really special, intentional candles.

When I start getting anxiety, which I often do, or have difficulty falling asleep, I do this breathing technique that activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which controls our “fight-or-flight” response: Breathe in through your nose for four beats, hold for seven, breathe out through your nose for eight beats. It calms my system down, and the anxiety goes away, or I drift off to sleep. I do it three to six times, sometimes a little more. But it always works for me.

#6872. Is Astrology Right or Left?

Like many aspects of American life, astrology is experiencing a political reckoning

In 1988, a former aide to President Reagan alleged that Nancy Reagan relied heavily on the astrologer Joan Quigley to guide key moments in her husband’s presidency. The Reagans denied that characterization, but the image of an easily swayed Nancy Reagan using some irrational force to push through Reagan’s policy decisions entered general pop culture, fueled in part by the misogyny of the political press toward most women in politics, left or right, in the 1980s.

The story of Joan Quigley and Nancy Reagan is part of a larger history of people turning to the stars to help explain, and potentially influence, political events. This is an impulse that doesn’t belong to any particular political ideology. But in current pop culture, astrology is often used as shorthand for liberal or leftist worldviews. Leading up to the 2024 U.S. presidential election, some online astrologers confidently celebrated a potential Harris win. Their despair over the actual outcome was reported in numerous outlets. It seemed to confirm the stereotype of who follows astrology: millennials who are deeply disturbed by the current state of the world.

Today, astrology, like weightlifting, holistic medicine, and organic farming, has become less a hobby and more a political signifier. So many practices that used to be read as lifestyle choices—tracking your macros, for instance, or taking up ceramics—have, in our hyperpolarized culture, become stand-ins for a person’s political leanings. But what’s curious about astrology is that it reads as liberal-coded to those outside the field and potentially conservative to those inside it.

In a 2022 piece titled “Astrology and the Search for Meaning” in The American Conservative, Bradford Tuckfield attributed the rise in astrology to the fact that cultural discourse had “damage[d] or destroy[ed] the …positive myths that used to animate people,” adding, “The patriotic idea of America as a great nation has been undermined by many efforts for decades, the most notable recent example being the ‘1619 Project.’ ” For conservatives like Tuckfield, astrology is an attempt to fill a vacuum left in the American psyche. The absence of religion and the demand that citizens grapple with uncomfortable historic truths supposedly left young people ideologically lost. “The myth of social justice seems to have absorbed much of the energy” of young people, Tuckfield claimed. And astrology is just like that “myth.” For conservatives, then, astrology is just one of many cultural challenges to a homogeneous culture built on the myth of the hero. Astrology, in their reading, leads to challenges to the status quo, which is, by definition, the antithesis of conservatism.

But that’s how outsiders view the field. Astrology also has its own organizational bodies that strive to define the discipline and often are administered by older, more politically and culturally conservative practitioners. But those groups’ influence over astrology has waned as it has become more accessible through the internet. Once-esoteric knowledge is now widely distilled through TikToks and live streams, and this has led to a political divide for many astrologers.

“To be able to study astrology historically has been a very privileged thing to do. And so it’s been dominated by white folks,” explains the astrologer Chani Nicholas. Nicholas is a popular astrologer who trained with those unaffiliated with traditional astrology organizations. “There’s very explicit reasons why I’ve not invested a tremendous amount of time in general astrology communities, even though I am massively indebted to the community and I have a deep appreciation for it,” she says.

Nicholas’s brand of astrology makes explicit connections between planetary transits and real-world political events. She directly cites progressive and leftist political goals in the context of astrology. Nicholas traces the political divide in astrology to post-pandemic America and links it to the same divide seen in alternative health spaces and yoga practices, where many practitioners became enthralled with MAGA and MAHA politics while others did not.

Before the pandemic, organized astrology was dominated by “a group of white people that are mostly able-bodied, mostly heterosexual, mostly cisgendered, that have never had to confront their own racism, sexism, homophobia, transphobia,” she notes. “In the pandemic, a lot of millennials started taking classes with a lot of establishment teachers, and a lot of the racism started to come out.… Millennials were like, ‘I’m not going to sit here and have you say that. You can’t say that.’ And then the more establishment teachers were like, ‘Why are you coming for me?’ ”

This tension between a more conservative old guard in astrology and a younger group of practitioners came to a head this summer when Stephen Forrest, an older practitioner of astrology, appeared on the Lila Astrology podcast. In the episode, he spoke about a satirical character he had once employed to make fun of racism, using the N-word in doing so. Forrest also brought up white-supremacist beliefs he had previously held on the inferiority of Black people’s intelligence; claimed to be related to Nathan Forrester, the founder of the KKK; and repeated the debunked myth that the KKK originally formed as a charity organization and not a terrorist one.

In response, younger astrology adherents asked that Forrest be removed as a speaker from an upcoming conference. Other older practitioners who were more deeply invested in astrology organizations cautioned against “cancellation.” Chani Nicholas, who was scheduled to appear at a conference that was also planning to host Forrest, pulled out of her appearance.

“Astrology attracts all these different types of people. It’s fascinating, but it doesn’t mean that because you’re interested in the human condition that you’re actually interested in interrogating your own biases and your own way of enacting violence on behalf of the system,” Nicholas says.

Astrology organizations, like many institutions currently, are caught between a younger generation, which is asking an older one to abide by the values of fairness and universal acceptance many purport, and older generations that are refusing to see the contradictions and biases in many of their organizations’ stances.

“There was no shared reality about where we had been working,” says Nicholas. “This younger generation of people that were really trying to understand identity and politics and location and power and privilege started to make this robust astrology community [of their own] and really push back against a lot of stuff that had just been existing unchecked for decades.”

Samuel F. Reynolds is an African American astrologer who, for more than 35 years, has chosen to work within astrological organizations. He originally came to astrology as a skeptic. One of the biases he pushes against is the tendency to ground all interpretations in Western culture. “When Uranus went into Libra, an older Western astrologer might say to you, ‘Oh yeah, that led to a time where there was an acceleration of divorces and where people started to gain more power related to marriage,’” Reynolds explains. “When they say things like that, I’m like, ‘Oh, okay, did that happen in Africa? And did that happen in India? Was that true in most of Asia?’ And the data actually is no, it didn’t happen during that same time frame.… It does bring up a philosophical question of what are we talking about and what are we looking at? Because the framework by which we talk about humanity is not quite as vast as astrologers like to believe when they talk about it. That’s the truth of it. You’re talking about the people who are like you or in some way who are adjacent to you.”

He remembers astrology conferences 20 years ago where “every other talk, someone would bring up the charts of the Beatles, Princess Di.” Astrology is an art of interpretation, which rests on the practitioner’s frame of reference. The limited references at conferences spoke to “the insularity in general of white people,” Reynolds says. He does note, though, that at a conference recently, he saw someone comparing the chart of Toni Morrison with Agatha Christie. “Why? I’m not exactly sure,” he jokes.

All of this conflict is happening in the wider context of big cultural and political shifts and the crumbling of any sort of authority or governing bodies. In extreme instability, the desire to understand why everything is changing makes astrology even more attractive. And astrology offers a lens for many to understand themselves. You may not be able to name why you have anxiety or why you tend to have chaotic romantic relationships or why you have trouble forming deep community bonds, but your planetary signature (and those of your friends and lovers) can provide you with something that looks like an answer.

But Nicholas cautions against giving too much sway to the question of transcendence and self-interrogation that astrology can sometimes provide. “I really want to talk about how we can build power and be effective now,” she says. “I don’t want to be out in outer space. I actually really want to be on earth.”

#6870. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6868. John Legend, Lynn Nottage, and Whitney White on Reclaiming an American Classic From a Black Perspective

The three artists have teamed up on a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life

In 2020, against the dual backdrops of the isolating Covid-19 lockdown and the righteous fury of Black Lives Matter protests erupting across the country, John Legend and Lynn Nottage began exchanging messages. Though the EGOT-achieving musician and the two-time Pulitzer Prize–winning playwright couldn’t meet in person, the pair quietly became collaborators on a long-gestating project, now finally reaching the stage: a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life.

For nearly a century, Imitation of Life—which began as a 1933 best-selling novel by Fannie Hurst, became a John M. Stahl melodrama the following year, then was adapted for the screen again by Douglas Sirk in 1959—has been a cinematic fixture in Black American households, though not an uncontroversial one. Stahl’s iconic telling centers on Bea Pullman, a white widow and single mother, who takes in Delilah, a Black single mother, as a maid in exchange for housing her and her light-skinned daughter, Peola (Sarah Jane in Sirk’s version). When Bea boxes Delilah’s family pancake recipe and puts a caricature of her likeness on the package—an unmistakable nod to Aunt Jemima—she becomes a wealthy businesswoman. But Delilah, content simply to care for Bea and her daughter, turns down any share of the money. Meanwhile, Peola, seduced by her adjacency to whiteness, wrenchingly disavows her mother to pursue a life of passing.

As Sterling Brown’s scathing and influential 1935 review for Opportunity: A Journal of Negro Life made plain, the novel and film are mired in racist tropes. Yet despite its flaws, the story has continued capturing Black audiences, perhaps for the complex humanity clearly latent within Delilah’s and Peola’s characters. Now the story is being retold in a limited-run production opening November 21 at the Shed in New York, this time with a fully realized Delilah as the lead and an all-Black chief creative team at its core. Nottage wrote the book, while Legend—who also recently released his 11th studio album, Muse—composed the entire score. Whitney White, of Jaja’s African Hair Braiding and Liberation, directs, with Adrienne Warren starring as Delilah and Jessica Vosk as Bea. Leading up to rehearsals for the show, Nottage, Legend, and White—so in demand that she was pulled into a production meeting midconversation—took time to discuss their own relationships to Imitation of Life, their experience working together, and why the story’s themes remain as relevant as ever.

Lynn Nottage: I come from a generation where both films, Imitation of Life from the 1930s and 1950s, were fairly ubiquitous on television. I ended up seeing the films probably once or twice a year and found myself in sort of a tense, love-hate relationship with Imitation of Life, in that I was frustrated by the treatment of Delilah and Peola in 1934, then once again by Delilah and Sarah Jane in the 1950s. And yet I was fascinated by the racial politics and this young woman who had a really angsty relationship with her own identity. So I felt, when I was invited to work on this, that I had an opportunity to delve deeper into the character of Delilah and right some wrongs. She is so fascinating; she’s a mother, she’s an entrepreneur, she’s a woman who defies the expectations of the time. And yet throughout history, she has not been given her just deserts.

John Legend: I didn’t grow up knowing this film, but my parents and their generation know it very well. So when I told them that I was involved in this musical, they were so excited. And it made me realize how important this work was in our culture. But like Lynn, it was a fun challenge to reimagine the storytelling, where Delilah was centered, her emotionality was centered, her humanity was centered, and Peola’s humanity was centered as well.

And then, of course, I’ve never written a musical before. So part of the joy and educational experience that I’ve gotten from doing this is working with people who have so much brilliance and experience and understanding of a medium that I didn’t bring to the table. It was the perfect introduction, for me, to writing a musical. It’s been such a welcoming and edifying way to enter into this new branch of my career.

Nottage: John is a natural in this medium. It’s almost as if he had gotten a master’s degree in musical-theater writing. I think that, John, you have so many musicals in you and the world is going to be blown away.

Legend: Thank you. It really did make me think, why hadn’t I done this before?

Whitney White: For me, I was raised by a single mom, and she raised me at great personal cost—spiritual cost—in Chicago, on her own. So the 1930s film, I oddly was more familiar with it because I liked it more. I would sit with my grandmother when she was having her coffee, like, at whatever-o’clock in the morning, because neither of us would ever sleep. And for some reason, I attached myself to the 1930s film. The visuals of that film are so iconic. It’s like the foundation of Americana as it exists in global culture is visualized in that film. But also the story of Delilah echoes in so many ways. It’s almost like touching a stove that’s too hot for me. Because I’ve watched, my whole life, the world not see my mother in this way: her brilliance, her intelligence, her spunk, her grit. So I just knew that I could bring something to it. But I also knew that John and Lynn were going to give that character the eulogy and the eternal life that she’s always deserved.

Legend: Yeah, I think because of Lynn’s perspective and storytelling, we see other sides of Delilah. We see her being romantic. We see her being a brilliant businessperson, making a way out of no way—something that a lot of our ancestors, especially, had to do. They did what they had to do to realize the American dream.

Nottage: At the core, this is a story about the American dream, with all of its contradictions and complications. It’s a story about motherhood; it’s a story about friendship, but it’s also a story about passing. It’s what we are forced to do to negotiate and survive in a world that is sexist, in a world that is racist. Why, in America, do we constantly have to make compromises in order to reach for the things that we desire? Those sacrifices, most often, people of color have to make, women have to make. We are often asked to reshape our identities to fit into some sort of template that’s not necessarily comfortable for the shapes and forms of our bodies and our ideas and our identities. I think that’s why we keep coming back to this story, because the struggle remains real.

White: And I think, in the midst of having to constantly reshape our identities and bodies as Black folk, at the same time, we got the goods. We make culture. Here you have this character who saves this business, and what does she make? Delicious food that is worth millions of dollars. So while we’re forced to be at the bottom of the totem pole, we also make the goods that make capitalism work. That’s something I’ve really been sitting with: We’re so often plundered and taken from the most, and yet we are the foundation of capitalism.

Nottage: Can I ask you, Whitney, you’re looking particularly luminous today. Where are you going?

White: Girl, I’m sweaty. I just walked my child [to school]. My child is very heavy. I have this giant toddler; maybe he’s going to be LeBron. He didn’t want to walk to school, so he made me push him in his stroller.

Legend: I met your husband last week, so I see where he got it from.

Nottage: I totally digress, but I was like, “Look at you, you have, like, a rosy green glow.” [Laughs]

White: [Laughs] That beat that just happened is so beautiful because it’s so indicative of the show. Like, we’re talking about this heavy stuff, and then we all have this beautiful laugh. When I read the text and hear the music, it’s like I go through 10 cycles of emotions. I laugh, I cry, I fall in love, then I laugh and cry and fall in love again.

Legend: One of my favorite things from the workshops is watching the music come to life. You have all these amazing performers embodying all the things that I wrote in my little piano room at home.

Nottage: Theater is often so difficult and fraught to make because it is collaborative. I think one of the things that’s been really lovely about this process is the ease with which we, as collaborators, are capable of having conversations, because there’s so much mutual respect and appreciation. The bottom line in making a musical is that trust is necessary. It’s a tremendous leap of faith.

White [as she is pulled into production meeting]: I just want to add one last thing. Oh God, I don’t want to get too emotional. Never in my wildest dreams did I imagine that I would be sitting here with two of my favorite living artists. When I started in this business, this kind of makeup for a musical team was impossible. I can’t tell you how much pride and excitement that brings me, to be working alongside John and Lynn.

Nottage: Thank you. We love you, Whitney.

White: I’m going to go lovingly yell at people—lovingly, lovingly. Bye.

Legend: Our writing process started—we were meeting during Covid, so a lot of our first interactions were on Zoom. Lynn would send me pages with story dialogue and possible lyrical ideas. I would sit in my piano room and sketch out an idea with maybe a couple lyrics, but mostly just mumbling musical ideas around the theme.

Legend: One of the things that happens more in musical theater than I’m used to is the revision process and the workshopping process. … I’ve definitely done more revision in this show than I’ve ever done in my pop-music career.

Nottage: John, do you remember one of the very first conversations that we had? I said, “We’re probably going to write that opening-number song at least 15 times.” You’re like, “Oh, I don’t think so.” Here we are!

Legend: Here we are. We’ve rewritten it multiple times. [Laughs] I don’t have an extensive knowledge bank of musicals that I’ve seen and been a part of. So it was interesting, thinking about how to write for one, because part of it was like, “Well, let me just bring my own musical experience in the church and popular music and, you know, singing in a jazz a cappella group and singing show tunes in the choir in high school.” I just brought my own experience and said, “Let me just write the songs that I can be proud of and that I believe will work.” And then Lynn really taught me how to incorporate the storytelling into the songs.

Nottage: It’s so interesting to hear you say that, John, because I think one of the things that made the process so smooth is that you are a natural-born storyteller. There’s something that you understand instinctively and fundamentally about dramaturgy that I didn’t have to explain to you. I would venture to say that perhaps musicals are the hardest form of theater-making just because it is so layered.

But I also want to circle back to something that you said earlier, John, about the beginning of the process. It was Covid, and we were all thinking and making in isolation. There was something really lovely about reaching across the country to John and having these conversations about a book, even though we didn’t really, at that point, know each other. We spent a lot of time talking about ideas. It was absolutely thrilling. When I got that first mumble, I was like, “Oh my God, it’s John Legend mumbling, and it still sounds beautiful.” It was a song that we never, ultimately, ended up using. I think that eventually you should do an album of the outtakes.

Nottage: I just want to add, because we’ve been talking about some heavy themes, that the musical is not delivering spinach. The musical embodies all of the complexities of a beautiful woman like Delilah, a beautiful woman like Bea, a beautiful woman like Peola. It really is, in some ways, a celebration. It is a love letter. And it is a gift to be able to be in dialogue with these women.

#6867. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6864. Time to Break Down That Major Twist at the End of ‘Campus Drivers’ and How It Differs From the Books

The Prime Video series takes a major turn from C.S. Quill’s series.

If—after watching the sixth episode of Campus Drivers—you wondered whether there were still more episodes to come, I’ve got bad news: That really was the finale. Season 1 of the French series from Prime Video ends with a twist that leaves plenty of questions, especially about Lane and Lois’s future. It’s a choice that even surprised those familiar with the book series by C.S. Quill, because the TV ending takes a different direction from the novel’s.

But what exactly happens in the final episode? And, above all, why does Lane decide to leave for Los Angeles right when his relationship with Lois finally seemed destined to take off? Let’s get into it.

In the season 1 finale, we discover that Lane leaves with his mother to move to L.A. and attend film school—a decision that, at first glance, seems to come out of nowhere, especially considering the bond that has formed with Lois over the course of the episodes. Behind this choice, however, there is a secret motivation: Lane’s mother does not approve of the path her son wants to take and believes his career choice is not the right one, but she accepts it on the condition that he leave Bellerose and end his relationship with Lois; otherwise, she is prepared to interfere with Lois’s scholarship, since she considers her an unsuitable partner for Lane.

At that point, Lane is faced with an impossible decision: stay beside the person he is falling in love with, or sacrifice his own happiness to allow her to continue studying medicine at a prestigious university.

Lane chooses the second option. He agrees to leave with his mother, thereby giving up the healthiest relationship of his life just when the two of them were finally beginning to truly grow closer.

The season’s final scene makes the separation even more concrete. In fact, we see movers carrying the furniture out of Lane’s apartment: an image that confirms his departure and the fact that the move to the United States is not just a threat or a possibility, but a decision that has now been made.

What’s more, Lois does not know the real reason for Lane’s departure. She does not know that he agreed to leave only to protect her future and allow her to continue her studies. In her eyes, that separation therefore risks seeming like a personal choice, a renunciation of their story just when it could have begun. At this point, the series departs considerably from the novel: the TV ending changes the protagonists’ journey and chooses to interrupt their story at a particularly delicate point, leaving the most important issue unresolved: the two protagonists did not get their romantic happy ending.

The ending seems designed precisely to leave the door open to a possible sequel (with the two of them still as major protagonists) and to heighten the drama—although for now, an official renewal has yet to be announced. Here’s hoping we will see Lane and Lois again in a second season!

#6862. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6859. Here’s How Much the Mirrorball Trophy Is Worth on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

We’re just a few weeks out from the Dancing With the Stars season 35 finale (it goes down on November 24), which means one couple is about to walk away with the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy. You know, the giant disco ball that everyone hoists above their heads after winning?

And if you’re wondering how much it’s worth, the answer is PRICELESS, obviously. But also, kind of a lot.

The mirrorball itself is incredibly cheap. Production designer James Yarnell told People in 2017, “No matter how much we put into the trophy, how much we spend, it always has a $10 mirrorball on top.” The show does throw down for the base, however. Winner Kaitlyn Bristowe told Trading Secrets that, all together, the trophy is worth around $10,000.

Apparently Hollywood 3D Printing—who uses “3D printing technologies combined with advanced finishing capabilities such as detailed painting, electroplating, and vacuum plating”—is responsible for making the Mirrorball Trophies, which they confirmed on Instagram in 2023.

Meanwhile, this glitzy statue has come a long way since the early days of Dancing With the Stars. Yarnell said he made the original Mirrorball Trophy (which Kelly Monaco won) from a brass lamp, a wooden base, and some tape. And it stayed that way for four full seasons! Per Yarnell, “They stopped making the lamp at Lamps Plus, so we’d run out of options and had to start casting it and put a heavy acrylic base with a brass stem.”

As for whether or not celebs and pros keep their trophies, it presumably depends on the person. But Derek Hough is so attached to his collection that he literally travels with them on tour and puts them in the lobby for fans to enjoy.

#6858. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6857. The 25 Sexiest Movies on Prime Video Right Now

Including the best erotic thrillers, raunchy comedies, and all the mess in between.

Even in just the past decade, sexy cinema has come a long way from Fifty Shades of Grey. No judgement here—the entire trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video if you haven’t seen the Dakota Johnson and Jamie Dornan-starring franchise. But if you’re looking to expand your range a bit, we have a lot to recommend.

Sexy movies are having a comeback in 2026, thanks to Wuthering Heights, I Want Your Sex, and—another Olivia Wilde film—The Invite. While Emerald Fennell adapted Emily Bronte’s novel into a tortured and depraved sexual experience, Wilde’s I Want Your Sex and The Invite used sex more as a vehicle for dark comedy. But it’s not just new films on this list. We can also point you to Weekend, Ghost, Before Sunrise, and just any kind of sexy movie you’re looking for on screen.

Below, you’ll find our pick of the 25 sexiest movies you can stream on Prime Video right now.

Olivia Wilde and Cooper Hoffman star in Gregg Araki’s raunchy workplace romantic comedy about a high profile artist (Wilde) who ropes one of her new assistants (Hoffman) into a dominant/submissive relationship. At first, he enjoys the attention, but the rollercoaster of their sexual power dynamic eventually drives them both insane.

Sydney Sweeney does Rear Window meets Peeping Tom in this sexy crime drama about a couple who becomes obsessed with the sex lives of the people they can see from their apartment window across the street. The film also stars Justice Smith and Ahsoka’s Natasha Liu Bordizzo.

No Hard Feelings follows a down-on-her-luck Uber driver (Jennifer Lawrence) who is hired by a very wealthy couple to take their inexperienced nineteen-year-old son (Andrew Barth Feldman) on a date to boost his confidence before he leaves for college. The film treads dangerous waters, but Lawrence won over the audience and earned herself a Golden Globe nomination for the sexy comedy.

Love her or hate her, Emerald Fennell can make a steamy movie. Wuthering Heights is a little more of a reimagining than an adaptation, taking quite a few liberties with the text, but it puts the romantic and sexual dynamics right at the forefront of the story. And there’s no beating Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi as the leads.

The Fault in Our Stars director Josh Boone teams up with It Ends With Us novelist Colleen Hoover for a romance drama about a teen pregnancy that alters the course of two lovers’ lives. That is, until later in life, when the couple (Allison Wiliams and Dave Franco) reunite to explore what could have been.

Emerald Fennell’s steamy The Talented Mr. Ripley–esque thriller, Saltburn, follows a young student at Oxford University who becomes infatuated (read: dangerously obsessed) with one of his aristocratic classmates, who invites him to his family’s grand estate. It’s a darkly funny and wildly intense ride.

The phenom that saved dark romance novels: Fifty Shades of Grey and the entire Fifty Shades trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video. The story, for those who still don’t know, follows a recent college graduate (Dakota Johnson) who signs a contract to take part in a sexual bondage relationship with billionaire Christian Gray (Jamie Dornan).

L’inconnu du lac (known as Stranger by the Lake) is one of the most acclaimed erotic thrillers of the 21st century, picking up Best Director and the Queer Palm awards in the year it premiered at the Cannes Film Festival. In Stranger by the Lake, Franck, a man in his 30s, meets Michel by a lake where gay men often bathe and immediately falls for him. What he doesn’t know, however, is that Michel is a psychopathic murderer. Stranger by the Lake is as smart as it is sexy, and it will truly keep you on your toes.

Who said rom-coms can’t be sexy too? Secretary charts the dominant-submissive relationship that blossoms between a lawyer and his secretary. It’s certainly on the crazier side and may not be for everyone, but Secretary is a brilliantly acted film that feels quite far ahead of its time for such a mainstream movie in its original take on a romantic BDSM relationship.

Carol does something a little different: It’s a slow-burning romance with sensuality bubbling beneath the surface. It follows a department-store worker and aspiring photographer whose life takes an unexpected turn when she falls in love with a mysterious, beautiful older woman going through a difficult divorce. Todd Haynes’s Oscar-nominated period romance is a masterpiece of modern queer cinema, with career-defining performances from Rooney Mara and Cate Blanchett. Hugely romantic and remarkably executed, Carol is a special film that’ll certainly stick with you.

Alongside Four Weddings and a Funeral, Sirens was one of the films that brought generational British heartthrob Hugh Grant to American audiences. Grant plays a progressive reverend who, with his wife, pays a visit to an eccentric artist whose work is viewed as blasphemous. His wife soon finds herself drawn to the three models working with the painter, and what ensues is a clever breakdown of 1930s sexual repression.

Everyone loves a meet-cute, and Weekend’s is as brilliant as they come. Russell thinks he’s signed up for a one-night stand with Glen, a man he meets in a club, but over the course of a single weekend, a deeper relationship quickly grows between the two. Touching, oddly realistic, and distinctly British in its sense of humor, Weekend is sexy, funny, and grounded in a way that few romantic comedy-dramas manage.

In Eternity, Elizabeth Olsen plays a woman who, upon reaching the afterlife, is forced to choose between two people to spend eternity with: her first husband, who died young, and her second husband of 65 years. Eternity is infectiously sweet, a little sexy, and an essential modern comedy.

It might be a stretch to call this one “sexy,” per se, but you won’t find a romantic drama quite like this one—and you definitely won’t find one that’s more intense. Luca Guadagnino’s Bones and All is a road-trip drama that follows a young woman who develops feelings for a disillusioned young drifter on a 3,000-mile journey across America. Oh, and they’re both cannibals.

Wong Kar-wai’s romantic masterpiece proves that a film can be truly romantic, sensual, and intense while still holding solidly on to a PG rating. In the Mood for Love follows a man and a woman who discover that their spouses are having an affair with one another, but as they bond, they begin to develop their own connection. This is one for the yearners.

Sexy films are by no means a new phenomenon, and Caligula is one of the originals. Though the original was pretty poorly received, the film recently received a restoration and rerelease that reconstructed the film entirely from footage that didn’t make the final cut.

Love triangles don’t get much more complicated than this, and sports dramas don’t get much sexier. Challengers follows two friends who fall in love with the same young tennis prodigy and the complicated chaos that ensues when their relationships get messy. Tennis has never looked so thrilling

Set in the dreamy Italian countryside, Call Me by Your Name remains the film that introduced Timothée Chalamet to the masses as a future leading man (and got him his first Oscar nomination). Here he plays a teenager who falls in love with his father’s assistant and comes to terms with his sexuality. The film is sad, symbolic, and deeply romantic.

The legendary leading duo of Patrick Swayze and Demi Moore speaks for itself. Ghost is maybe a little more on the sentimental side than some of the other “sexy films” in Prime Video’s lineup, but it’s a deeply moving one worth your time.

Portrait of a Lady on Fire was an immediate hit with critics and audiences when it was released in 2019. The film follows an artist commissioned to paint an aristocratic heiress, but things become more complicated when the two fall in love. Romantic films don’t get more French than this one, which is also loosely based on the timeless tale of Orpheus and Eurydice.

Nicole Kidman and Harris Dickinson are an absolutely electrifying onscreen pair in this erotic thriller that sees a CEO begin an affair with her power-hungry intern, putting the stability of her family life and career on the line. Kidman picked up the Best Actress award at the Venice Film Festival for her role, and it’s been called one of her most ambitious performances to date.

Can anything capture your heart like a meet-cute romance? American traveler Jesse and French student Céline unceremoniously meet on a train from Budapest to Vienna, deciding on a whim to spend his last hours on the continent together. It’s the first in Richard Linklater’s acclaimed Before trilogy, and it sets you up for a brilliant slow burn.

If a Skarsgård signed up for it, I’m interested. He’s about to play a love interest made out of wicker, but last year Alexander Skarsgård played the mysteriously sexy biker-gang leader Ray in Pillion. The romantic black comedy tells the story of a shy man who begins a dominant-submissive relationship with the aforementioned biker, and it traces his personal growth as he navigates their dynamic. Pillion is sexy, crazy, and downright hilarious. It’s British black comedy at its finest.

Starring the king of crazy, surprisingly sexy indie movies, James Spader himself, Sex, Lies and Videotape is a true cult classic. The film revolves around a married couple with intimacy problems whose lives are changed when a mysterious drifter with a peculiar interest comes to stay with them.

David Cronenberg’s notorious erotic thriller is perhaps the most transgressive entry on this list, following a group of people who become sexually fixated on car crashes. Adapted from J.G. Ballard’s novel and starring James Spader, Holly Hunter, and Deborah Kara Unger, Crash is disturbing, provocative, and about as daring as erotic cinema gets.

#6856. The ‘Campus Drivers’ Finale May Have Revealed Who Season 2 Will Be About, and It’s a Change From the Books

Lois and Lane’s unfinished story could signal a major shift.

The season 1 finale of Campus Drivers left viewers with several questions, one of them being: Who will the protagonists of the next season be? Prime Video’s French-language series is based on the book series by C.S. Quill, but it has already demonstrated that it doesn’t necessarily intend to follow the novels’ path to the letter. And the very way Lane and Lois’s story ends could foreshadow an important narrative choice for the future of the adaptation.

As a reminder: In the series finale, Lane decides to leave for Los Angeles with his mother in order to allow Lois to keep her scholarship. Their story is cut short just when it seemed destined to turn into something more, leaving several questions open that could become central in a second season.

However, season 1 does also end with a twist involving Donovan. Without giving too much away about what happens in the second book, Crash Course, which is centered on Donovan, the ending sees (spoiler!) Donovan’s dad collapse during a basketball practice. Still, Lois and Lane remain at the center of the narrative with a story still entirely unresolved…and this choice may not be accidental.

The choice to keep Lane and Lois at the center of the story could suggest that Campus Drivers will not necessarily follow the narrative model of Off Campus. In the series based on Elle Kennedy’s novels, each season is designed to mainly tell the story of a new couple, giving space to different protagonists within the same narrative universe.

For Campus Drivers (despite the series having the same structure), the direction could be different: keeping Lane and Lois as central figures in the coming seasons as well, gradually developing the love stories of the other characters and giving them more and more space. Based on the season 1 ending, it’s possible that it will instead use an increasingly ensemble structure, in which the protagonists’ stories intertwine and become richer episode after episode, without necessarily making way for a new main couple each season.

This does not rule out the possibility that, further down the line, other characters could take on an even more important role. As early as the third season, for example, there could be room to explore the secondary love stories in greater depth. For now, however, these are only hypotheses; Prime Video has not yet confirmed what the structure of any future chapters will be.

If the series is renewed, it is reasonable to expect Lane and Lois to maintain a central role. The first season finale left their relationship hanging, and it would be difficult to imagine a follow-up that does not pick up precisely from what happened between them—especially because six episodes felt like too few to fully develop a dynamic between the leads.

The choice not to follow the structure of the novels could also be a way to differentiate Campus Drivers from Off Campus, especially since both series share the same platform and target the same audience of fans.

At the moment, a second season has not yet been officially confirmed. However, considering the attention the series is receiving and the narrative potential left open by the finale, it would not be surprising to see new updates arrive soon.
